The 2028 Primary Has Begun. Sorry.

We’re about to spend a year arguing about Democrats’ plans. I’d like to hear how they’ll actually make them happen.

Jon Favreau

Nov 14

Preview

READ IN APP

The 2028 Democratic primary has begun.

I know. They’re still counting votes from the last election. But the people who are thinking about running for president are already hiring staff, making calls, and visiting early states. If history is any guide, we’ll probably get the first few announcements right after the holidays. Candidates will then have about a year to impress us before the first votes are cast in South Carolina.

It’s hard to overstate the importance of the decision we’re about to make. It’s also hard to overstate how unbelievably stupid primaries can get. The last time we did this, weeks of our lives were consumed with questions of great national consequence, like: should the Democratic nominee be allowed to stand on tables? Is holding a fundraiser in a wine cave disqualifying? What about allegedly using the phrase “horse-faced lesbian” several decades ago? What was Joe Biden’s position on busing in the 1970s, and how much did Kamala Harris charge for the t-shirt that memorialized their dispute?

Then came approximately five hundred debates about Medicare for All, Medicare for All Who Want It, Medicare for All Who Can Afford It, and Medicare Whether You Want It or Not. The eventual winner went on to the White House and delivered Medicare For The Same People.

I wish I could tell you 2028 will be different. But some of the nastier primaries of 2026 don’t exactly inspire confidence. And the internet hasn’t exactly become a more thoughtful place to work through our differences… ...

NEWSLETTER · 14 Nov 2026, 17:03

😸 Deep Dive: The Future of Work is Multiplayer

PLUS: What happens when AI stops being your personal assistant and starts working alongside the whole team? ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌

November 14, 2026 | Listen online | Read online

Welcome, humans.

Today's special edition asks a simple question: What happens when AI stops being your personal assistant and starts working alongside the whole team?

Right now, you can ask one AI to research a project, another to write a proposal, and a third to build an app. The outputs might be excellent. But you're still the person explaining the assignment three times, transferring files, and reconciling conflicting answers.

Congratulations! You've become the world's most overqualified copy-and-paste machine.

That's the problem behind multiplayer AI. Businesses depend on shared decisions and coordinated work, yet most AI experiences still revolve around one person and one conversation. What changes when humans and agents can contribute to the same project?

Want to Sponsor a Deep Dive like this? Advertise in The Neuron here!

Everyone has an AI assistant. Nobody has an AI team.

Think about your last big project. How much time went toward the work itself, versus chasing updates, finding documents, and repeating context?

Microsoft's 2025 Work Trend Index found that its most heavily interrupted users received an average of 275 meeting, email, and chat notifications across a 24-hour workday. That's roughly one interruption every two minutes during core working hours.

At that point, your actual job is apparently the side quest.

Personal AI assistants can summarize messages or draft replies. But picture three teammates using separate assistants. One knows the customer research, another has the marketing plan, and a third knows the deadline changed. Unless that information travels between them, the humans still have to keep everyone synchronized.

Individual productivity isn't the same as team productivity. Multiplayer AI aims to give people and agents shared context, clear responsibilities, and a common place to move work forward.

FROM OUR PARTNERS

Good ideas could use some company.

A project moves faster when everyone can work together, including the AI agents helping get things done.

Builderlab brings colleagues, agents, and business tools into one workspace, letting companies build workflows around their own processes instead of adapting everything to fixed software.

Teams can use agents and models out of the box, bring their own setup, or combine both approaches. Remote agents can continue handling tasks after laptops close.

And with pay-as-you-go pricing and no per-seat charges, businesses can bring in the people they need without adding another license for every colleague.

Bring your team

Let’s build an AI-powered team.

Imagine a small company launching a subscription service in three weeks. Four employees handle marketing, operations, support, and project management. Three AI agents help with research, planning, and content.

This is an illustrative scenario, not a documented Builderlab customer deployment.

Monday: The project lead sets a launch goal, budget, deadline, and customer profile in a shared workspace. The research agent reviews customer feedback. The planning agent maps tasks and dependencies. The content agent drafts launch emails and website copy.

Tuesday: The research agent notices customers care most about one feature. It adds the evidence to the shared project. The content agent can use the finding without someone pasting it into a new chat.

Meanwhile, the planning agent notices that marketing wants to announce the service before operations has finished onboarding. It surfaces the conflict. The operations manager changes the timeline, and the new decision becomes part of the project context.

Wednesday: The content agent drafts an email promising immediate access. Operations hasn't approved that claim. A human catches the mistake, confirms the actual launch schedule, and asks for a revision.

Friday: Customer support joins the project. Instead of a lengthy briefing, the specialist can review the decisions, evidence, and outstanding tasks.

That's the promise: one team's work becomes usable context for the next task. Agents handle execution, while humans resolve ambiguity, check claims, and authorize consequential decisions.

Compare that with three private chatbot windows. The research agent's finding would need to be exported, explained to the content agent, and checked against the planning agent's timeline. If the launch date changed, somebody would have to repeat that update everywhere. A genuinely shared workflow could carry the update forward once, with the understanding that permissions and data quality still determine whether the result is trustworthy.

A shared workspace doesn't guarantee that the information is correct. But it can reduce the manual work of reconstructing what everyone knows.

An AI teammate changes more than the workload.

There's reason to think AI can change how expertise gets combined.

In a 2026 Organization Science field experiment involving 791 Procter & Gamble professionals, people working individually with AI produced product-innovation solutions comparable in quality to two-person teams working without it.

Researchers also found that AI helped participants incorporate perspectives beyond their specialties. Technical employees considered more commercial concerns, while commercial employees drew on more technical ideas.

That's valuable because teams exist partly to combine expertise. The research doesn't prove AI can replace departments or that autonomous agent groups perform as well. It does suggest that AI can help individuals cross familiar boundaries.

But an AI assistant isn't automatically a useful teammate. To participate in coordinated work, an agent needs four things:

Shared context: The goal, latest information, and previous decisions.

Ownership: A defined task and someone accountable for the outcome.

Tools and permissions: Access to what it needs, but not unrestricted authority.

Continuity: A record of what's finished, blocked, or awaiting approval.

Without those ingredients, several smart agents can produce several incompatible answers. An agent that completes the wrong task quickly hasn't saved anyone time.

There's also a technical difference between sharing a folder and sharing working context. An agent needs to know which version of a document is current, whether a proposed change was approved, and which task depends on another. Simply giving several agents access to the same files doesn't answer those questions. The workspace needs a way to represent project state: what is proposed, what is confirmed, and what remains unresolved. Humans need to be able to correct that state, too.

The challenge is making coordination part of the workflow rather than another job for the human supervising it.

FROM OUR PARTNERS

Adapt your workspace to your business.

Every company has its own processes. Why should every team have to work the same way?

Builderlab's Buzz brings people, agents, and projects into a shared environment. Teams can build and share applications for recurring work and use plugins to customize how their workspace looks and functions.

Buzz is also open source, allowing organizations to inspect, extend, and host the workspace themselves.

That gives businesses more flexibility to decide which tools and models they use, how their workflows operate, and how much of the underlying setup they want to manage.

Get started

More agents don’t automatically mean better results.

If one agent helps, wouldn't five be better? Not always.

A 2026 Nature Machine Intelligence study tested 260 multi-agent configurations and found that adding agents didn't consistently improve performance. When a single agent was already capable, coordination could add overhead instead of value.

It makes sense. Put five people on a one-hour assignment and you may get more meetings than progress.

Multiple agents make more sense when work splits into distinct responsibilities, such as researching, planning, and drafting. But every handoff creates a chance for an error to spread.

Back to our subscription launch: Suppose the research agent misreads customer feedback. The content agent turns that finding into a confident promise, and the planning agent builds a schedule around it. Now three agents agree on the same mistake.

Agreement isn't validation.

Research on multi-agent systems has identified miscoordination and conflict as potential risks. In ordinary business settings, failures may be simpler: stale information, duplicated tasks, an accidental disclosure, or an automated process continuing after plans change.

The answer isn't to demand human approval for every tiny step. It's to set boundaries proportionate to the decision.

A useful safeguard is to distinguish preparation from commitment. An agent may collect information, compare options, or prepare a draft inside a permitted workspace. Sending information outside the company, changing a customer record, or spending money should require explicit authority. Teams also need a trail showing which inputs the agent used, what it changed, and who approved the result. Otherwise a fast workflow can become a fast-moving mystery when something breaks.

An agent can draft a customer email. A person should approve the promise before it goes out. An agent can research vendors. Someone with spending authority should approve the purchase.

Useful delegation means agents can act within clear limits, humans can inspect their work, and consequential decisions have accountable owners.

What if the team becomes the interface?

Most businesses fit their work around software: projects in one tool, conversations in another, documents somewhere else. People become the connectors.

Multiplayer AI suggests another approach: Start with the work, then arrange people, agents, and tools around it.

Our fictional launch team might need a project dashboard, a customer-feedback workflow, and an approval process for marketing claims. Another organization would need different tools and rules.

That flexibility could change three things.

The difference is organizational, not merely technical. Teams could build a repeatable process once, then let people and agents reuse it without starting from a blank prompt each time. A customer-support workflow might pull approved product information, draft a response, and flag unusual cases for a specialist. A financial workflow could prepare a reconciliation but require a controller to approve the final numbers. Each business chooses which steps can run automatically and where judgment belongs.

Smaller teams could draw on more specialties. As the Procter & Gamble experiment suggests, AI can help individuals consider unfamiliar perspectives. Four employees still won't have the judgment of forty, but they may be able to evaluate more options before bringing in specialized expertise.

Managers may spend more time defining work. Delegation requires clear goals, reliable sources, permissions, and success criteria. When agents can execute quickly, a vague assignment can produce a bad result at machine speed.

Turns out, moving faster in the wrong direction still gets you lost.

Project knowledge could become a team asset. Instead of decisions buried in personal chats, the team could retain research, approvals, open questions, and the reasons behind a change. That context is only useful if it's accurate, current, and visible to the right people.

And none of this means every task needs an elaborate agent orchestra. Sometimes one person and one well-equipped assistant are enough. The point is to match the collaboration to the work.

FROM OUR PARTNERS

Start small.

You don't need to redesign your entire company to explore multiplayer work.

With Builderlab, you can create an organization, invite colleagues by email, and bring people and agents together in one workspace.

Start with one team and a recurring project. See which handoffs could become simpler, where agents can help, and which decisions still need a person in charge.

Get started with Builderlab

The Future of work needs a better definition of teamwork.

The most effective AI-powered company probably won't be the one running the most agents. It'll be the one that divides work sensibly without losing shared understanding or human accountability.

The test should be practical. Pick one recurring team project and ask:

How often do people transfer information between tools?

How much context must they repeat?

Where do handoffs cause delays or mistakes?

Can the team finish with fewer coordination steps and the same quality?

Those questions tell us more than an agent count.

The promise of multiplayer AI is that people and agents can contribute to the same work without humans constantly stitching disconnected systems together.

The real test isn't whether the agents can work together. It's whether the humans can work better because of it.

That’s all for today!

How to Shut Down Trump’s Pardon Abuse

A three-part agenda for exposing corrupt deals, challenging blanket pardons, and recovering stolen money.

Amanda Carpenter • FREE PREVIEW

READ TO ME

(Photo illustration by Bill Kuchman/The Bulwark | Photos: x.com/WhiteHouse, Justice.gov, Getty, Shutterstock)

FACING AN ONSLAUGHT of investigations and oversight from a new Democratic Congress come January, Donald Trump appears to be drastically escalating his pardon offers.

The president recently said he plans to issue “tremendous numbers of pardons,” possibly to any allies “within a thousand miles” of the Oval Office—a considerable geographic markup from his earlier musing that he might pardon those within “200 feet of the Oval.”

I beg your pardon?

Voters just delivered a stinging rebuke of Trump in the midterms, sending an unmistakable signal of their disgust over the administration’s corruption, yet the president shows no signs of correcting course. This week, the White House issued a fresh round of potentially unconstitutional threats to prosecute Democrats for planning to conduct oversight of the administration. At the same time, Trump is keen to continue abusing the pardon power to insulate his political cronies from the legal consequences of their criminal behavior.

We are getting a clear picture of Trump’s twisted two-tiered justice system: legal harassment for those who challenge him and a free pass for those who win his favor. But a new Congress is coming to town, and it’s time for the days of fixers getting paid, criminals getting clemency, and victims getting stiffed to come to an end.

While the Constitution does grant the president expansive pardon powers, the Founders would be horrified at a president using them as a tool for open corruption. We don’t need to wait for a constitutional amendment to start reining in these abuses. Congress can put the brakes on Trump’s pardon abuse with a three-pronged strategy: ...

Open-source AI without Jensen

A decapitation. It’s time to rebuild.

Nathan Lambert

Nov 14

READ IN APP

Listen to post · 7:28

The resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is a major setback for the US development of an open model ecosystem. Nvidia still has the financial interests in supporting open models, which are to avoid concentration in power among a few customers who would develop their own chips and reduce Nvidia’s profits, but supporting the open ecosystem is a tricky puzzle more than an engineering problem to grind out a solution on.

Nvidia’s business operations are famously extremely dependent on Jensen as an operator, so it is a consensus opinion that there are challenges ahead (or even more high-profile departures through chip-smuggling investigations or plain-old sharks circling more poachable talent). Nvidia’s number one goal is to stabilize the ship, deliver to customers in the next 24 months, and prevent implosion. Open models have been one of Jensen’s more ambitious, founder-style bets. He has been flooding the zone with funding in a way that will very likely dry up.

Interconnects AI is a reader-supported publication. To receive new posts and support my work, consider becoming a free or paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

In this period, the open model economy is in its nascency. These are crucial years for establishing use-cases, revenue flow, and motives to continue training the next generation models. I discussed the sensitivity of these early years in my recent talk at Fireworks Forge (slides, recording). The core of the argument is one upside for open models, a reason to be optimistic, and one downside.

The positive case is that the open model economy has correctly latched onto “ownership of intelligence” and enterprise post-training as a crucial lane. What is to be determined is exactly how big the downstream market is.

The downside is that the true long-term benefit of open models, as truly commoditized intelligence, is very far from realization with compute in such short supply. In the near term, where inference startups are also struggling to get enough chips (because Anthropic and OpenAI are buying so much capacity), there’s little margin competition on open-weight models. If open-source models are also served at giant inference margins, as we learned with DeepSeek’s DeepInfer release, they’re far from a commodity. In this paradigm, the closed labs with better economies of scale and integration from model to datacenter and chips will win. I expanded on this challenge in my recent piece on the open model economy.

Together, a lot still needs to go right for open models to be a meaningful economic engine. Jensen’s support, and protection, gave the ecosystem time to figure this out.

Jensen was more than a cheerleader. He would stop a lot of shit from going wrong once early winds were stirring. He was willing to use his power on open models.

There’s a major swell in Democratic government funding towards candidates that want to regulate the AI industry, fostering “safety”. Many of these people feel a need to do something and have general opinions that open models are unsafe, while not being educated on the complexities of their benefits. My biggest near-term worry is sloppy regulation at the state level, followed by national regulation. We’ve been one, strong Chinese open-weight model away from a crisis since this summer. It still holds – especially as we enter a new, turbulent equilibrium of cybersecurity. We have not seen a major hack driven by a popular Chinese open-weight model, but it is a matter of when, not if. We need someone to encourage caution and an ecosystem perspective when the “open-weight hawks” come hunting.

Knocking over the open-source AI domino today is easy — if done, unwinding the downstream concentration of power, reduction in safety research, and stifling of long-term innovation is far harder. On public data, it looks like balancing long-term innovation versus short term risks, but this is due to how horribly hard it is to measure the open model economy (e.g. the most valuable aspects of it run on private infrastructure). It’s a recurring challenge facing the ecosystem and my work.

As a brief aside, the extent to which the media ecosystem is revolving around timing today, rather than exact availability of information, is incredible. All of these Jensen affairs were known, but getting them out at the right time, right after the midterm political swing, took down the king.

The same goes for the Jacob Coxon brouhaha this summer. All of that safety culture around top labs was well known, but the right timing around datacenters and the Overton window made it a national issue. This could very well happen at some point for open model safety, as it tends to be harm, risk, or safety-related narratives that drive the modern media algorithms. I cannot imagine my work on open-science driving such a media cycle, because it will never happen.

Share

The only lasting solution to this is to incubate a truly frontier open model player in the US. For example, I agree completely with Dylan Patel, CEO of SemiAnalysis, on a core problem in the US open model ecosystem. From a recent ChinaTalk + SemiAnalysis emergency podcast:

Jordan Schneider: I do want your guys’ take on just how bad this is, that Jensen’s now off the table for the future of American innovation. And we should talk about open models. I mean, he was the big champion, right? Dylan, you were talking earlier — I still don’t understand quite why someone didn’t just spend a few billion dollars and make something better than Kimi or DeepSeek and open source it.

Dylan Patel: Not that easy. It’s not money, it’s talent, man. All of the most talented people primarily work at Anthropic, OpenAI, DeepMind, and Meta TBD, right? And then those who don’t are split across a hundred different startups. I mean, yes, Thinking Machines has a cracked team, but is Thinking Machines as good as Moonshot? No. Moonshot skill-wise is probably just as good as the US labs. They just have a big deficit in compute and deployment. So I think if we don’t have a scale advantage, then you don’t win. Yes, these smaller labs have spent a lot of money on models. But DeepMind, Grok, Meta have regularly fallen behind the frontier of Chinese labs, and they’re spending more. So it’s not just, oh, spend a few billion dollars and you can make a model that’s better.

The best people for attracting talent are the ones at the front of movements. Jensen helped catalyze this, and on many metrics was still losing. Nemotron has had the right level of ambition and strong people, but it hasn’t delivered to the point of being called “mission accomplished.” The competition here in building leading-edge models is more fierce than most give credit to. Having an organization with billions of dollars, solid people, and good vibes may not get you there. You normally need something special.

It could be the case that Thinking Machines has gotten to maturity in its commitment to open models, with its recent Inkling 2 Small being the best Western open-weight model in a while. Still, it’s far behind Kimi K3.1 Pro and expectations are high for Zhipu’s next GLM update. It’s a better sign that we’re at least having this debate, but the open ecosystem is riddled with questions and answers like this – maybe they can pull it off? On the other side of the equation, closed labs like Anthropic look like some of the greatest businesses ever assembled, which inspires confidence in the longevity of their approach.

Who is Mira going to call when they need to close their next funding round? Supposedly the next round at a $40B valuation is a done deal, but that won’t be enough to keep pace with the closed frontier for long.

The fundamental problem is that the open-weight ecosystem needs someone who will pick up the phone to abate a crisis. There are more challenges ahead. The most likely allies we could turn to are from the hyperscalers, the likes of Zuckerberg, Andy Jassy, or Satya. With such a coordinated campaign of pressure from the AI safety community that has open models as a negative target, I worry that being proactive is the only way to stay ahead. Jensen has carried that mantle for about two years, and times were messy before he stepped up clearly in 2025. We’re regressing in the operational capability of our movement, when the stakes and attacks are destined to rise. We open model believers are due some soul-searching and coalition building to construct a new platform. Shoes have been emptied that will never fully be refilled.

Ukraine war briefing: US proposes Kyiv cede land to Russia for Nato membership, Zelenskyy says

Ukraine war briefing: US proposes Kyiv cede land to Russia for Nato membership, Zelenskyy says. Ukrainian president says Kyiv not ready to concede Donbas but would put idea to a referendum in exchange for security guarantees and funding. What we know on day 1,726 Volodymyr Zelenskyy has said the US has asked for the first time if Ukraine would withdraw from parts of the eastern Donbas region that Russia does not control in exchange for Ukraine becoming a Nato member But the Ukrainian president said his country would not surrender territory. “The Americans raised a question: ‘What if Ukraine joins Nato? Would Ukraine withdraw from Donbas?’,” he said in remarks to reporters released by his office on Saturday. “That is why I believe Nato is back on the agenda.” But he said the suggestion, which would form part of a new postwar security framework for Europe, could allow Russia to weaken Nato and reshape borders. “This is dangerous because, in my view, it is precisely things like this that lead to wars or escalations,” Zelenskyy said. Zelenskyy reiterated his position that Kyiv was not ready to concede territory but said that if it received a comprehensive package including security guarantees, the right to a strong and well-funded Ukrainian army and funds to rebuild, he would put the question to a national referendum. Echoing comments made by European leaders at a summit in Dublin on Friday, Zelenskyy dismissed Russia’s call for a broad agreement on European security architecture to be reached before the specifics are agreed on how to end the war in Ukraine. “We cannot turn everything upside down and start by talking about changing the security framework in the world or across the continent overall, and only then talk about ending this war. This war must be ended first,” he said. The Ukrainian president has also rejected the possibility of Ukraine committing to neutrality , something that could only happen if the country’s constitution was changed. “If Putin expects Ukraine’s neutrality to be enshrined, that’s not going to happen,” he said. “We’re not in Nato right now anyway.” US President Donald Trump’s envoy and son-in-law Jared Kushner said on Saturday the US was focused on facilitating direct engagement between Russia and European nations on the Ukraine war , after trilateral meetings involving Washington, Moscow and Kyiv this week. However, Washington and Nato have not yet commented on the suggestion of a swap of the Donbas for Ukraine Nato membership. Negotiations did, however, lead to agreements that European countries would join future talks on ending the war. Zelenskyy has accused Space X of not telling the truth over restrictions to Ukraine’s Starshield satellite system , saying the decision to impose speed limits appears to be “political” rather than “technical”. Speaking on Friday, Ukraine’s president said the restrictions affecting Ukrainian drones, in place for five days, meant they could no longer intercept incoming jet-power.

Why kimchi maker Daesang bets on movies

Why kimchi maker Daesang bets on movies. Korea's kimchi and traditional sauce giant Daesang is thinking outside the box to promote its products globally — turning to movies instead of conventional food marketing. The company's Eat & Travel Film Festival (ETFF), which held its sixth edition in August, is attracting growing interest from international participants and overseas consumers. Held for first time in Europe this year, the festival brought together 48 students, half of them from 24 countries, who spent two weeks engaging with locals and tourists in Budapest while promoting the event and serving free Korean dishes made with Daesang's products. Eo Ho-kyung, brand communication team manager at Daesang Holdings who coordinates the festival every year, has renewed her perception of foreigners’ positive reception of K-food and Korea during this year's event. Why Anthropic's blockbuster IPO is getting a muted reception in Korea

Why Anthropic's blockbuster IPO is getting a muted reception in Korea. Anthropic's planned initial public offering (IPO) is drawing a muted response in Korea despite expectations it could raise a record-high $90.4 billion, as local brokerages and asset managers shy away from the deal following fallout from Space X's IPO, industry officials said Sunday. There were no Korean brokerages among the 22 underwriters named in Anthropic's S-1 registration statement filed with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. Korean retail investors are therefore unlikely to gain access to shares through the IPO. Those in Korea will still be able to invest through exchange-traded funds (ETFs). Major asset managers, including Mirae Asset, KB, NH-Amundi and Samsung Asset Management, have launched a range of related ETFs since news of Anthropic's IPO plan emerged. SK chairman calls for AI expansion beyond chips into models, services

SK chairman calls for AI expansion beyond chips into models, services. SK Group Chairman Chey Tae-won urged leaders of group subsidiaries to redefine themselves as artificial intelligence (AI) companies and expand beyond chips, infrastructure and energy into AI models and services, building a growth cycle that connects the conglomerate’s businesses. According to SK Group, Sunday, Chey outlined the strategy at CEO seminar held Thursday to Saturday in Icheon, Gyeonggi Province. During the seminar, Chey highlighted the importance of an "AI flywheel" to position SK as a key player in a “token economy,” where AI computing and services are traded as resources. “We must break out of existing business frameworks and redefine ourselves as AI companies,” Chey said, urging every affiliate to find new roles beyond its traditional business and connect them to AI services and the token economy. Under the strategy, AI semiconductor capabilities would support AI factory expansion, supporting the growing demand for electricity, energy and AI services. Competitive energy supplies and wider service adoption would spur investment in AI factories and advanced chips.

HD Hyundai launches autonomous vessel developed with Anduril

HD Hyundai launches autonomous vessel developed with Anduril. South Korean shipbuilder HD Hyundai said Sunday it has launched an autonomous surface vessel (ASV) jointly developed with U.S. defense firm Anduril Industries as it seeks to expand into the U.S. market for unmanned naval vessels. HD Hyundai and Anduril held a launching ceremony at HD Hyundai Heavy Industries' shipyard in Ulsan, about 310 kilometers southeast of Seoul, on Thursday. The 600-ton ASV is equipped with VAone, an autonomous navigation solution developed by HD Korea Shipbuilding & Offshore Engineering, and Anduril's Lattice platform, enabling it to conduct reconnaissance, surveillance and maritime intelligence-gathering missions without a crew. HD Hyundai plans to conduct sea trials off Ulsan before further testing in the U.S. early next year to gather operational data as it seeks to enter the U.S. Navy's unmanned vessel market. The U.S. Navy is pursuing a hybrid fleet of manned and unmanned vessels to strengthen its maritime capabilities. In September, it announced plans to procure 30 medium-sized ASVs. LG Electronics' Tennessee plant tops 10 mil. units in cumulative production

LG Electronics' Tennessee plant tops 10 mil. units in cumulative production. LG Electronics’ Tennessee plant surpassed 10 million units in cumulative production, playing a pivotal role in helping the company secure the top spot in the U.S. laundry appliance market. The company said Sunday it held a ceremony to congratulate the production of the 10 millionth unit at the plant participated by local officials and LG Electronics executives. LG Electronics built its Tennessee plant on a 1.25 million-square-meter site in Clarksville, Tennessee in 2018. Since then, the company has invested approximately $500 million in expanding facilities and improving manufacturing processes. The plant began producing washing machines in November 2018, followed by dryers in 2022 and wash towers in 2023. Its annual production capacity now stands at 2.25 million units, more than four times its 2019 level, comprising 1.55 million washing machines and 700,000 dryers. The expansion has established the plant as LG Electronics’ key local manufacturing and supply hub. LG Electronics’ washing machines and dryers accounted for 17.2 percent and 17.4 percent of the U.S. market, respectively

Kia's domestic electrified vehicle sales top 300,000 units in 2026

Kia's domestic electrified vehicle sales top 300,000 units in 2026. Kia said Sunday its domestic sales of electrified vehicles surpassed 300,000 units this year as of Monday, making it the first automaker to reach the milestone in Korea in a single year. Kia, an automaking affiliate of Hyundai Motor Group, said its sales of electrified vehicles, including electric vehicles (EVs) and hybrid models, came to 300,048 units, up 41.9 percent from a year earlier. Annual sales of electrified vehicles first topped 100,000 units in 2021, reaching 109,741, before exceeding 200,000 in 2024 at 222,085. Of this year's tally, EVs accounted for 120,360 units, more than doubling from a year earlier and marking the first time an automaker has sold more than 100,000 EVs in Korea in a single year. Sales of hybrid models over the period rose 16.9 percent to 179,688 units. Kia added that its overall domestic sales this year came to 502,286 units, with electrified models accounting for 59.7 percent of the total. Audi Korea accelerates recovery on solid sales of redesigned A6

Audi Korea accelerates recovery on solid sales of redesigned A6. Audi Korea surpassed 10,000 units in cumulative sales early this month, driven by solid sales of its redesigned A6 flagship sedan and expanded vehicle lineups with diverse powertrains, the carmaker said. The sales figure was achieved a month earlier than last year, marking the second consecutive year that the German automaker has hit the 10,000-unit sales milestone in Korea. Audi Korea is on track for a rapid sales rebound here after it revamped its overall strategy with a focus on product portfolio expansion. The carmaker introduced 16 new models last year, including flagship all-electric models — the Q6 e-tron and A6 e-tron — as well as the A5 sports sedan and Q5 SUV with internal combustion engines. The A6 sedan is particularly the key sales driver for Audi Korea, with the model's sales reaching 2,241 between January and September here, according to the carmaker. The strategy is designed to give Korean customers a broader range of choices amid rapidly diversifying preferences for powertrains, body styles and driving needs. The FAA’s IT troubles are far bigger then AI

The FAA’s IT troubles are far bigger then AI. U.S. airlines are still reeling from a nationwide ground stop issued on Friday that interrupted weekend travel and wreaked havoc on schedules. The culprit, the Federal Aviation Administration says, was simply outdated tech. The ground stop was followed by eager speculation on social media about what might have caused the issue. Some pointed a finger at a new AI system that the FAA integrated earlier in the week; others blamed foreign adversaries. At the same time, a range of tech companies that contract with the FAA, like Microsoft ,were quick to distance themselves from the agency’s problems. Later Friday evening, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy told reporters the outage occurred after an old analog system called time-division multiplexing became overloaded. This is an arcane system of processing data signals over a network. The overload, in turn, impacted the telecommunications systems used by terminal radar approach controls, or TRACONs, the air traffic control facilities that help direct flights at airports. Duffy noted that systems that had already been upgraded based on internet protocols did not experience the outage. “This is all the old legacy equipment,” he said “All of the new equipment that’s been deployed did not go down.” Officials, at least for now, insist there’s no evidence anything nefarious happened. Instead, the Department of Transportation is, once again, begging Congress for more money to overhaul FAA tech. Duffy urged Congress to provide additional funding to modernize the nation’s air traffic control system, arguing that the issue should transcend partisan politics. He noted that the FAA had received $15 billion for modernization efforts over the past 25 years, with little to show for it. “It’s not red, it’s not blue, it’s not Democrat, it’s not Republican, it’s American,” he told reporters. “Give us the money to fix it.” Indeed, the FAA’s tech problems are way bigger than AI. Instead, they have a lot more to do with the challenges of updating technology while it’s being used. The pilots guidance system is ancient and isn’t designed to handle upgrades This isn’t the first time the FAA has experienced a major outage that’s brought flights across the country to a halt. Back in 2023, the Notice to Airmen system (NOTAM), which alerts pilots to potential hazards in American airspace, went offline after contractors accidentally deleted files, disrupting its core technology. There’s been an effort to improve the NOTAM system, but it’s moving at a snail’s pace. The FAA has brought in private industry for help and engaged in an effort to modernize the system, but that process has only brought to light myriad other problems. For one, at least according to contract paperwork obtained last year , NOTAMs weren’t exclusively being stored in a virtual environment or in an entirely digital format. Another aspiration the FAA was working on, at least at the time: making sure that.

Show HN: A terminal-native idle factory game, written in Rust with ratatui

Show HN: A terminal-native idle factory game, written in Rust with ratatui. Hey HN readers! . I'm snipedbywifi , and i've been working on terminactory. Please try it out if you'd like to play a cozy factory game while waiting for long agent tasks to finish! . The game is a factory game (overused niche, i know x D), but runs in your terminal. Reason i built it? glad you asked: So basically, it started off with me wanting a terminal-native factory game. Me and the modern 5.5 claude models made a base for the game that I liked, and then i went ham adding features / fixing some minor flaws (like adding claude code hooks so you recieve "telegrams" [the agent's responses], so you can review progress directly in-game). (yes, I had heavy AI assistance, disclaimer!!!) Right now, the game has basic factory features you'd expect from a game like this (build stuff, delete stuff, measure how much you're earning, basic selection tools), along with a few more. "unexpected" features. This includes a fake in-game browser that has a wiki, fake forums, some fun bits of lore, some more lore, a LOT more lore, and a research graph that shows how many items or how much RP (research points) you'd need to unlock something, and some more QOL features. If anyone actually has this issue / want of a terminal-based game that runs alongside your agents so you don't doomscroll, do you have any suggestions, or additional ideas I could add? . It's hosted on Git Lab, soo, that's one additional thing i'd like to mention Thanks for reading!! snipedbywifi (p.s. first HN post lol) (p.s. p.s. there's a lot of hidden lore in the game, so. lore-divers? you might like it?) Did Alice Huang's Megaspeed Bypass America's Controls on Exporting Nvidia Chip to China?

Did Alice Huang's Megaspeed Bypass America's Controls on Exporting Nvidia Chip to China?. In three years "Megaspeed International" went from from inactive Chinese gaming subsidiary "to one of Asia's biggest buyers of cutting-edge semiconductors from the US," reports CNN. As Nvidia's Jensen Huang had an affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, "Megaspeed quietly instilled billions of dollars of Nvidia chips in data centers across Southeast Asia," CNN writes, noting scrutiny of the company from the Singapore government and US officials "concerned that the company was passing those chips on to unauthorized Chinese buyers. " In 2022, the US began restricting sales of semiconductors to China, including Nvidia's most advanced. While the controls were meant to curb China's tech industry, they also spawned opportunities for intermediaries willing to help circumvent them. US officials believe Megaspeed may be one of those. [T]rade records show Megaspeed has purchased more than $4 billion in chips and equipment from Nvidia. Based on indictments and investigative reports, research institute Epoch AI estimates that anywhere between 290,000 to 1.6 million advanced US semiconductors were sent to China in violation of export controls last year. Singapore also initiated investigations into Megaspeed and its controllers, including Alice Huang, for potential false declarations relating to the company's server purchases and their final destinations. In a statement, Singapore's Ministry of Home Affairs said there was insufficient evidence that Huang had committed any related offenses, but she had been barred from re-entering the country after providing inaccurate information "to downplay her role" at Megaspeed. The other investigations are ongoing. The exact scale of Chinese chip smuggling is impossible to quantify. However, its existence has become something of an open secret in the industry, as well as an indication that US efforts to keep adversaries from securing high-stakes technology still have fundamental flaws. A May report from the short seller Culper Research claimed that despite tightening trade restrictions, Chinese end-users still account for about 20% of Nvidia chip sales via illegal diversion and intermediaries in Southeast Asia. CNN reminds readers that Jensen Huang accompanied US President Trump to events with Chinese leader Xi Jinping twice this year, and also lobbied for the US to relax controls on Chinese chip imports. "The US government ultimately approved several buyers for less-advanced Nvidia chips, known as H200, while collecting a quarter of the revenue." "However, blacklisted companies have continued to obtain sensitive semiconductors as well," they add. And the Taiwanese government is even seeking to criminalize unauthorized exports of advanced AI chips to C.
RSS · 14 Nov 2026, 23:37

Manjaro Linux Moves Toward Independent Community Governance

Manjaro Linux Moves Toward Independent Community Governance. Arch-based Manjaro Linux "has announced that Manjaro Project e.V. is now officially registered as a non-profit in Germany," reports Linuxiac, "and is therefore a legally separate entity from the commercial Manjaro Gmb H & Co. KG. after months of internal wrangling over who was in charge of the project, who had access to the infrastructure and who didn't, and the relationship between the dev team and the commercial company. " The restructuring goes back to March, when the Manjaro developers released the Manjaro 2.0 Manifesto, which was a very public critique of the way Manjaro was being run. The document claims that decision-making has effectively become the sole domain of the project founder, Philip Müller, with the other developers having very limited access to the infrastructure. There's also a concern about the drop in participation, the persistent technical issues, and the continued prioritization of commercial interests over the actual development of the distribution. The manifesto proposed the transfer of all project infrastructure, domains, repositories and community funds to the new organization, together with arrangements for the use of the Manjaro trademark. The contributors were warning that they might well consider forking the distro if the talks didn't go their way. Müller then indicated that he'd be happy to talk about the proposed structure once the non-profit was in place. The association is now formally registered, and so one of the key aims of the manifesto has been realized. Perhaps the most important point is the actual evolution of the distribution. The most recent testing and stable updates were kicked off by the Manjaro team themselves, without any oversight from Müller. This points to the developers having already started to operate on their own in an area which was previously the preserve of the project's founder. They're also in the process of optimizing their dev infrastructure, in part as a response to the continued automated bot attacks on the existing infrastructure. Thanks to Slashdot reader king*jojo for sharing the news. of this story at Slashdot. .

EFF Criticizes US Government's Use of AI For Evaluating Senior Health Insurance Requests

EFF Criticizes US Government's Use of AI For Evaluating Senior Health Insurance Requests. "When we need medical care, we expect decisions about our treatment to be made by our doctors, not a tech company's algorithm," writes the EFF newsletter EFFector. But in January America's federal government launched a program called WISe R that uses AI to evaluate authorization requests for certain procedures from America's federal health insurance program for seniors, Medicare. EFF sued for records and discovered "additional issues" with the program that they say "could jeopardize Medicare beneficiaries' access to care." The records we received revealed that WISe R has resulted in widespread delays and denials of care, operational chaos, and reports of patient harm. Further documents obtained by EFF show a rushed and rocky rollout, highlighting the dangers that arise when governments treat AI like a silver bullet for complex problems. EFF has now published hundreds of pages of documents obtained through our lawsuit, available here. The documents also highlight the need for more transparency about safeguards against inappropriate, AI-driven denials. EFF has long argued that AI has no place in consequential government decisions about peoples' rights or access to benefits, such as immigration enforcement and policing decisions. That's because using automated decision-making in these contexts risks decisions that violate people's rights or deprive them of benefits they're entitled to based on biased or inappropriate considerations and without proper due process or accountability if there is an error. By outsourcing decision-making to private AI companies without sufficient transparency, testing, or legal limits, WISe R jeopardizes seniors' access to medical care. Congress should step in to place greater legal limits on the use of automated decision-making in Medicare. The new documents "show why we need stronger legal limits around government use of automated decision-making. " according to the newsletter. And another EFF page argues that the American public "still lacks the information needed to assess whether clinician review is providing meaningful oversight of AI systems and whether appropriate data is being used to evaluate prior authorization requests." WISe R's " [One company] denied more requests than it approved in the first few months of the program, and CMS inquired about "denials that appear inaccurate or inconsistent with applicable criteria." Ultimately, the records obtained by EFF raise more questions than they answer about the safeguards used by WISe R vendors. A growing body of scholarship points to the substantial limitations of "human in the loop" policies as guardrails for AI systems. AI-generated recommendations can unduly influence human decisions, leading reviewers to over-rely on automated judgements and rubber stamp them as correct. Even worse, at least one health insurance company actively pressured clinical reviewers to accept i.

Hundreds of Fake Accounts Defended Jensen Huang On Social Media

Hundreds of Fake Accounts Defended Jensen Huang On Social Media. The Washington Post reports on something peculiar about support for Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang online after he'd told the world he'd been unfaithful to his wife (before reports of his second extramarital affair): Joining the chorus online were voices of seemingly everyday people: a chef, a stock trader, a Hebrew teacher and several environmental activists. But at least 400 of them were fake, according to research from the nonprofit Midas Project, with links to a public relations firm involved in previous so-called "sockpuppet" campaigns. Many of the fake accounts, according to Midas Project executive director Tyler Johnston, replied to journalists, former U.S. officials and other prominent figures and backed not just Huang but Nvidia's push to sell its cutting-edge chips to Chinese firms. Like other documented influence campaigns, the posts appeared to have limited reach; the most-viewed tweet garnered about 8,000 views and 32 likes. Still, some of the posts targeted lawmakers, including accounts for the [U.S.] House Select Committee on China, Republicans on the House Foreign Affairs Committee and Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Massachusetts). "That's a place where you might not need a lot of views, but maybe the staffers who are running the lawmakers' account are paying attention to how their messages are received," said Johnston, whose group advocates for safe AI development. There were other clues that helped identify accounts as being part of an apparently coordinated campaign, according to the Midas Project. Some of them were created minutes or hours apart, and had user names and profile photos refreshed at similar times. They also sometimes engaged on oddly specific issues ranging from Qatar to Nippon Steel's bid for U.S. Steel and a waste management plant in Oregon. Any one one of the posts — often replies to other posts — might have not looked suspicious in isolation. But the various clues taken together, Johnston said, gave evidence that they were linked. Some of the accounts then rushed to delete posts after he posted his findings online, and at least 260 of them have been deactivated or suspended by X. of this story at Slashdot. .

Amazon Expands Drone Deliveries, and Gets Noise Complaints. 'Like a Swarm of Bees'

Amazon Expands Drone Deliveries, and Gets Noise Complaints. 'Like a Swarm of Bees'. "In August, Amazon announced it was expanding its airborne shipping to suburban areas in nearly 500 U.S. cities," reports the Chicago Sun-Times, "including the Chicago, Atlanta, Cleveland and Boise, Idaho, metro areas." And on Tuesday Amazon cut the ribbon on its new drone delivery center Tuesday 24 miles south of Chicago. But Amazon's drones are already servicing parts of Texas, notes the Houston Chronicle. And north of Houston, Theresa Gibbons complains "It was kind of cool and fascinating at first, but now it is driving me crazy." Her home in the Bridgestone neighborhood is nestled right in the flight path of Amazon's drone service. Gibson said she hears roughly a dozen drones flying over her home each day. Noise pollution has become a frequent complaint in Texas neighborhoods where the service operates. Residents have compared the sound to everything from a massive beehive to a full-on air raid. "It does not sound like a [single] bumble bee. It sounds like a large swarm of bees at a much higher volume than a normal swarm of bees. And it's all day long," said Gibson. Amazon's drone delivery service is currently operating in four Texas cities, including Houston, Waco, San Antonio and Richardson. The company announced major expansion plans in August, but growing complaints in pilot neighborhoods could foreshadow complications later down the line, with regions such as Harris County already taking legal action in response. So far, a group of fifteen states, plus Harris County, [Texas] have formed a coalition that has filed a petition in the US Court of Appeals challenging the Federal Aviation Administration's environmental review, citing that it failed to adequately address the effects of expanding drone deliveries. And meanwhile near Boise, Idaho, local news station KTVB reports. Less than two weeks after Amazon began delivering packages by drone in Nampa, the cities of Nampa and Meridian say they have received complaints over the noise and how often the drones fly. Amazon began Prime Air drone deliveries Oct. 30 from its East Franklin Road fulfillment center, making Nampa the company's first Idaho drone site. How people feel about the drones depends on whom you ask. A Meridian man who has received drone deliveries says the noise doesn't bother him. Neighbors closest to the launch site say the drones fly from sunup to sundown and are disturbing their peace. Randy Nye, who has lived in Cherry Creek Estates for 17 years, compared it to living in a beehive. "It's just a constant presence," he said. Nye, who works from home, said he and his neighbors who technically are just outside city limits had no idea this was coming. He said the city approved the drones in May, and they were never given written notice this was coming, despite living so close. He said they were shocked when deliveries started by drones just 12 days ago. Nye said he hopes something can change.

And as to the cause of the incident. [U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean] Duffy said a router involved in the switch from copper wire to fiber "started flooding other routers in our air traffic control system." The system saw "over 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds," he said. That disrupted communications with airplanes and radar. America's nationwide grounding of flights on Friday happened "amid growing scrutiny. of a new AI-powered air traffic management system" deployed by America's Federal Aviation Administration, reports Texas news station KXAN. Ironically, that system is called SMART — but the outage happened "following warnings" from members of the U.S. Congress about this new AI system's safety, reliability and oversight: In an Oct. 13 letter to the Government Accountability Office, more than 20 members of Congress requested an independent review of the FAA's development and implementation of its Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes and Trajectories system, known as SMART. The lawmakers questioned whether the technology was being deployed with adequate input from frontline air traffic controllers, sufficient training and appropriate safety evaluations. In their letter to the GAO, the lawmakers specifically questioned whether the FAA had adequately evaluated risks associated with SMART's artificial intelligence capabilities, including the potential for the technology to interfere with experienced controllers' decision-making or generate inaccurate safety-critical information. "The nation's frontline aviation safety workforce apparently had little to no input into SMART's development and have received limited training and information during the current deployment process," the lawmakers wrote. The letter also questioned the FAA's decision to initially pilot the technology in heavily congested airspace, including the National Capital Region. "Our switches, our routers, actually got overloaded with data," U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy acknowledged Friday. But FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford said Saturday that officials have found "zero evidence of anything AI related" in Friday's outage, according to Yahoo News. An article from The Hill.com notes that Duffy also said the new AI tool had nothing to do with the ground stop, and that he said the system "isn't plugged in." "This is completely disconnected, and I love that some people have said this would be the root cause. By the way, if it was, I'll tell you that, but this is not even plugged in," he said. "So, there's no possible way SMART would have impacted." Duffy also asked Congress for $50 billion to rebuild what he described as an "incredibly old" air traffic control system. Linus Torvalds Wonders If Linux's AI-Prompted Patch Flood Has Peaked

Linus Torvalds Wonders If Linux's AI-Prompted Patch Flood Has Peaked. The ." In his weekly kernel update post, Torvalds said Linux 7.4 rc2 was a big release partly because it contained code he'd left out of rc1 because he'd been busy and it was already the largest such release in kernel history. But Torvalds said the other reason rc2 was big was "various AI fixes." Such fixes are necessary because ever since Anthropic launched its Mythos bug-finding model in April, developers have been able to find more software flaws than was previously possible. Microsoft, for example, addressed a record 974 flaws in September — its third record fixing spree this year after it whacked 622 flaws in July and 206 in June. Oracle addressed 1,449 flaws in a single month. That's many more patches than IT teams are set up to handle, yet they must find ways to deal with bugs more quickly because attackers are increasingly targeting flaws found by AI. Torvalds' post suggests that, perhaps, the worst of this is behind us. "This time around Alexei claims that on the bpf side at least the fixes are now coming in faster than the new reports, and that sounds lovely if true," he wrote. That's a reference to Alexei Starovoitov, co-creator and lead dev on the Extended Berkeley Packet Filter (e BPF), the project that makes it possible to run sandboxed programs within the Linux kernel. "I think most other areas haven't reached that point yet, but it's at least a sign that we may be starting to see the tide starting to turn," he added. The patchpocalypse has already affected Linux kernel developer Lorenzo Stoakes, a principal software engineer at Arm, The He explained: "Given the current throughput in the kernel resulting from AI-generated series, I unfortunately lack the bandwidth to generally be able to offer review except in those areas I maintain. [. ] Apologies for doing this, but given the current trends I'd like to do something to make my inbox a little more manageable." Stoakes told The And yeah, AI is part of the reason; in general, it's gone from 'once a week' to 'daily' to 'half of what I see in review'." of this story at Slashdot. .
